					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">An Archangel responsible for healing in most Abrahamic religions</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Raphael_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Raphael (disambiguation)">Raphael (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a></div><br /><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Raphael</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><span class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Frameless"><a href="/wiki/File:Saint_Raphael.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Saint_Raphael.JPG/220px-Saint_Raphael.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="326" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Saint_Raphael.JPG/330px-Saint_Raphael.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/97/Saint_Raphael.JPG/440px-Saint_Raphael.JPG 2x" data-file-width="619" data-file-height="916" /></a></span><div class="infobox-caption"><i>Saint Raphael the Archangel </i> by <a href="/wiki/Bartolom%C3%A9_Esteban_Murillo" title="Bartolomé Esteban Murillo">Bartolomé Esteban Murillo</a></div></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Archangel, "Angel of Tobit", Angel of the Trumpet</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Christianity" title="Christianity">Christianity</a><br /> (<a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Catholic_Churches" title="Eastern Catholic Churches">Eastern Catholic Churches</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Oriental Orthodox Church">Oriental Orthodox Church</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Churches</a><br /><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>)<br /><a href="/wiki/Islam" title="Islam">Islam</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style><div class="plainlist">
<ul><li><b>29 September</b>; <b>24 October</b> (local calendars and by those observing the 1921–1969 <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a>)</li>
<li><b>3 <a href="/wiki/Intercalary_month_(Egypt)" title="Intercalary month (Egypt)">Kouji Nabot</a></b>;<sup id="cite_ref-auto_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <b>13 <a href="/wiki/Koiak" title="Koiak">Koiak</a></b> (Coptic Churches)</li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Saint_symbolism" title="Saint symbolism">Attributes</a></th><td class="infobox-data">holding a bottle or flask; walking with <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Tobit" title="Book of Tobit">Tobias</a>; sounding a trumpet; carrying a fish or a staff</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">Patronage</a></th><td class="infobox-data">travelers; lovers; the youth; finding one’s spouse; ordained marriage; mental health; healing; guardian angels; shepherds; pharmacists; druggists; nurses; physicians; illness; eye afflictions; the sick; the blind; against nightmares; Diocese of Madison, WI; Archdiocese of Dubuque, Iowa; Archdiocese of Seattle, Washington; <a href="/wiki/Abra_de_Ilog" title="Abra de Ilog">Abra de Ilog</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mindoro_Occidental" class="mw-redirect" title="Mindoro Occidental">Mindoro Occidental</a>, <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>; <a href="/wiki/Aloguinsan,_Cebu" class="mw-redirect" title="Aloguinsan, Cebu">Aloguinsan, Cebu</a>, Philippines</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Raphael</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><small><a href="/wiki/British_English" title="British English">UK</a>: </small><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">RAF</span>-ay-əl</i></a>, <span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><small><a href="/wiki/American_English" title="American English">US</a>: </small><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span><span title="/ə/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;about&#39;">ə</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span></span>,<span class="wrap"> </span><span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/eɪ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;face&#39;">eɪ</span><span title="&#39;f&#39; in &#39;find&#39;">f</span></span>-/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling"><span style="font-size:90%">RA(Y)F</span>-ee-əl</i></a>; "God has healed")<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> is an <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangel</a> first mentioned in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Tobit" title="Book of Tobit">Book of Tobit</a> and in <a href="/wiki/1_Enoch" class="mw-redirect" title="1 Enoch">1 Enoch</a>, both estimated to date from between the 3rd and 2nd century BCE.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoogan1993642_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoogan1993642-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarnes199354_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes199354-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> In later Jewish tradition, he became identified as one of the three heavenly visitors entertained by <a href="/wiki/Abraham" title="Abraham">Abraham</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Oak_of_Mamre" title="Oak of Mamre">Oak of Mamre</a>. He is not named in either the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> or the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, but later Christian tradition identified him with healing and as the angel who stirred waters in the <a href="/wiki/Pool_of_Bethesda" title="Pool of Bethesda">Pool of Bethesda</a> in John 5:2–4,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoogan1993642_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoogan1993642-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> and in Islam, where his name is <a href="/wiki/Israfil" title="Israfil">Israfil</a>, he is understood to be the unnamed angel of <a href="/wiki/Sura_6#Notable_verses" class="mw-redirect" title="Sura 6">Quran 6:73</a>, standing eternally with a trumpet to his lips, ready to announce the <a href="/wiki/Day_of_Judgment" class="mw-redirect" title="Day of Judgment">Day of Judgment</a>. In <a href="/wiki/Gnostic" class="mw-redirect" title="Gnostic">Gnostic</a> tradition, Raphael is represented on the <a href="/wiki/Ophite_Diagrams" title="Ophite Diagrams">Ophite Diagram</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Origins_in_post-exilic_literature">Origins in post-exilic literature</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Origins in post-exilic literature">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>In the <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_Bible" title="Hebrew Bible">Hebrew Bible</a>, the word <span title="Hebrew-language text"><span lang="he" dir="rtl">'מַלְאָךְ'</span></span> (<span title="Hebrew-language text"><i lang="he-Latn">malʾāk̠</i></span>) literally means messenger;<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> either human or supernatural in nature. When used in the latter sense it is translated as "<a href="/wiki/Angel" title="Angel">angel</a>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMeier199947_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeier199947-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> The original mal'akh lacked both individuality and hierarchy, but after the <a href="/wiki/Babylonian_exile" class="mw-redirect" title="Babylonian exile">Babylonian exile</a> they were graded into a Babylonian-style hierarchy and the word <i>archangelos</i>, <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangel</a>, first appears in the Greek text of 1 Enoch.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrossman201152_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrossman201152-10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVan_Henten199981_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVan_Henten199981-11">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> At the same time the angels and archangels began to be given names, as attested in the Talmudic statement that "the names of the angels were brought by the Jews from Babylonia",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrossman201151_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrossman201151-12">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> attributed to <a href="/wiki/Shimon_ben_Lakish" title="Shimon ben Lakish">Shimon ben Lakish</a><sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> or <a href="/wiki/Haninah_ben_Teradion" title="Haninah ben Teradion">Rabbi Hanina</a><sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> respectively.
</p><p>Raphael first appears in two works of this period, 1 Enoch, a collection of originally independent texts from the 3rd century BCE, and the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Tobit" title="Book of Tobit">Book of Tobit</a>, from the early 2nd century BCE.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEsler20173_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEsler20173-15">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESoll20001110_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESoll20001110-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> In the oldest stratum of 1 Enoch (1 Enoch 9:1) he is one of the four named archangels, and in Tobit 12:11–15 he is one of <a href="/wiki/Seven_Archangels" title="Seven Archangels">seven</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBarker2006123_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarker2006123-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>  
</p><p>His name derives from the Hebrew <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_root" class="mw-redirect" title="Hebrew root">root</a> רפא (<i>r-p-ʾ</i>) meaning "to heal",<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> and can be translated as "God has healed".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMach1999688_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMach1999688-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> In Tobit he goes by the name Azariah (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: עֲזַרְיָה/עֲזַרְיָהוּ <i>ʿĂzaryāh/ʿĂzaryāhū, "</i><a href="/wiki/Yahweh" title="Yahweh">Yah/Yahu</a> has helped")<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> while disguising himself as a human. In the text he acts as a physician and expels demons,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMach1999688_19-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMach1999688-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> using an extraordinary fish to bind the demon <a href="/wiki/Asmodeus" title="Asmodeus">Asmodeus</a> and to heal Tobit's eyes, while in 1 Enoch he is "set over all disease and every wound of the children of the people",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMach1999688_19-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMach1999688-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> and binds the armies of <a href="/wiki/Azazel" title="Azazel">Azazel</a> and throws them into the valley of fire.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESoll20001110_16-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESoll20001110-16">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_post-biblical_Judaism">In post-biblical Judaism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: In post-biblical Judaism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg/220px-Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="184" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg/330px-Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5d/Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg/440px-Rembrandt_Abraham_Serving_the_Three_Angels.jpg 2x" data-file-width="539" data-file-height="450" /></a><figcaption><i>Abraham with the Three Angels</i> by <a href="/wiki/Rembrandt" title="Rembrandt">Rembrandt</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>According to the Babylonian <a href="/wiki/Talmud" title="Talmud">Talmud</a>, Raphael (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: <span lang="he" dir="rtl">רְפָאֵל</span> <i>Rəfāʾēl</i>, <a href="/wiki/Tiberian_Hebrew" title="Tiberian Hebrew">Tiberian</a>: <i>Răp̄āʾēl</i>) was one of the three angels who appeared to <a href="/wiki/Abraham" title="Abraham">Abraham</a> in the oak grove of <a href="/wiki/Mamre" title="Mamre">Mamre</a> in the region of <a href="/wiki/Hebron" title="Hebron">Hebron</a> (Genesis 18; Bava Metzia 86b);<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a>, as the greatest, walked in the middle, with <a href="/wiki/Gabriel" title="Gabriel">Gabriel</a> to his right and Raphael to his left (Yoma 37a).<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Each was commanded to carry out a specific mission, Gabriel to destroy <a href="/wiki/Sodom_and_Gomorrah" title="Sodom and Gomorrah">Sodom</a>, Michael to inform <a href="/wiki/Sarah" title="Sarah">Sarah</a> that she would give birth to <a href="/wiki/Isaac" title="Isaac">Isaac</a>, Raphael to heal Abraham from his recent <a href="/wiki/Circumcision" title="Circumcision">circumcision</a> and save <a href="/wiki/Lot_(biblical_person)" title="Lot (biblical person)">Lot</a>. <a href="/wiki/Rashi" title="Rashi">Rashi</a> writes, "Although Raphael's mission included two tasks, they were considered a single mission since they were both acts that saved people."<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Life_of_Adam_and_Eve" title="Life of Adam and Eve">Life of Adam and Eve</a></i> lists him with the archangels Michael, Gabriel, <a href="/wiki/Uriel" title="Uriel">Uriel</a> and Joel, and the medieval <a href="/wiki/Jewish_philosopher" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish philosopher">Jewish philosopher</a> <a href="/wiki/Maimonides" title="Maimonides">Maimonides</a> included his name in his <a href="/wiki/Angels_in_Judaism#Angelic_hierarchy" title="Angels in Judaism">Jewish angelic hierarchy</a>.
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Smaller_midrashim" title="Smaller midrashim">Midrash Konen</a> it is revealed that Raphael was originally once named Libbiel<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: לִבִּיאֵל <i>Lībbīʾēl;</i> Meaning: "God is my heart"). In the Midrash, <a href="/wiki/God_in_Judaism" title="God in Judaism">God</a> takes council with His <a href="/wiki/Angels_in_Judaism" title="Angels in Judaism">Angels</a> before he creates <a href="/wiki/Adam_and_Eve" title="Adam and Eve">Adam</a> the first Man. The Angels were not all of one opinion however, with differing views and reasons. The Angel of Love and Angel of Justice both favoured Man's creation as he would be affectionate and loving, alongside practicing Justice. The Angel of Truth and the Angel of Peace opposed his creation however, as he would be full of lies and be quarrelsome. To invalidate his protest, God cast the Angel of Truth down from Heaven to Earth, and when the others cried out against the treatment of their companion, He said, "Truth will spring back out of the earth." Before their objections, God had only told the Angels of the good there would be among Humans, but not of the evil too. Despite not knowing the whole truth, the Angels were nevertheless prompted to cry out: "What is man, that Thou art mindful of him? And the son of man, that Thou visitest him?" God replied: "The fowl of the air and the fish of the sea, what were they created for? Of what avail a larder full of appetizing dainties, and no guest to enjoy them?" And the Angels could not but exclaim: "O Lord, our Lord, how excellent is Thy name in all the earth! Do as is pleasing in Thy sight." For not a few of the Angels their opposition bore fatal consequences. When God summoned the band under the Archangel Michael, and asked their opinion on the creation of man, they answered scornfully: "What is man, that Thou art mindful of him? And the son of man, that Thou visitest him?" God thereupon stretched forth His little finger, and all were consumed by fire except their Chief Michael. And the same fate befell the band under the leadership of the Archangel Gabriel; he alone of all was saved from destruction. The third band consulted was commanded by the Archangel Libbiel. Taught by the horrible fate of his predecessors, he warned his troop: "You have seen what misfortune overtook the Angels who said 'What is man, that Thou art mindful of him?' Let us have a care not to do likewise, lest we suffer the same dire punishment. For God will not refrain from doing in the end what He has planned. Therefore it is advisable for us to yield to His wishes." Thus warned, the Angels spoke: "Lord of the world, it is well that Thou hast thought of creating man. Do Thou create him according to Thy will. And as for us, we will be his attendants and his ministers, and reveal unto him all our secrets." Thereupon God changed the Archangel Libbiel's name to Raphael, the Rescuer, because his Host of Angels had been rescued by his sage advice. He was appointed the Angelic Prince of Healing, who has in his safe-keeping all the celestial remedies, the types of the medical remedies used on Earth.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Tanhuma" title="Tanhuma">Midrash Tanhuma</a>, <a href="/wiki/Satan" title="Satan">Satan</a> becomes envious of the righteous R. Matthew bar Heresh after seeing him sitting occupied in <a href="/wiki/Torah" title="Torah">Torah</a> study, without looking at anyone's wife or any other woman. Believing it to be impossible for a righteous man to exist in the world without sin, Satan asks <a href="/wiki/God_in_Judaism" title="God in Judaism">God</a> how He views Rabbi Matthew; He sees him as completely righteous. Satan then asks for permission to test R. Matthew, which is granted to him. Satan then takes the form of a beautiful woman upon finding the <a href="/wiki/Rabbi" title="Rabbi">Rabbi</a> studying Torah. After seeing that Satan would continue to try and tempt him from all sides; he used hot pins to blind himself lest his <a href="/wiki/Yetzer_hara" title="Yetzer hara">evil inclination</a> prevail. Satan then trembled in dismay and reported back to God. Immediately upon hearing this, God called Raphael, Prince of the Healing Arts, commanding him to heal the eyes of R. Matthew bar Heresh. When Raphael goes to R. Matthew and reveals his identity and mission, the Rabbi states that he does not wish to be healed. Raphael then returns to God informing Him of this. Upon hearing this God commands Raphael to tell the Rabbi not to fear, for his evil inclination will not prevail. When he heard this from the mouth of the angel, he accepted his healing and was not afraid.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In Rabbeinu Bahya, a commentary on the Torah written by <a href="/wiki/Bahya_ben_Asher" title="Bahya ben Asher">Rabbi Bahya ben Asher</a> (1255–1340), the Camp of <a href="/wiki/Tribe_of_Ephraim" title="Tribe of Ephraim">Ephraim</a>, situated to the west of the <a href="/wiki/Tabernacle" title="Tabernacle">Tabernacle</a> (Numbers 2:18),<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> corresponded to the celestial camp headed by the archangel Raphael supported by the angels Zavdiel and Achziel. It is also said that this was the camp that <a href="/wiki/Moses" title="Moses">Moses</a> alluded to when he prayed that <a href="/wiki/Miriam" title="Miriam">Miriam</a> be healed from her <a href="/wiki/Tzaraath" title="Tzaraath">tzaraath</a> by saying "please God heal her" (Numbers 12:13). He appealed to the attribute represented by Raphael.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is said in <a href="/wiki/Kav_ha-Yashar" title="Kav ha-Yashar">Kav HaYashar</a> by Rabbi <a href="/wiki/Tzvi_Hirsch_Kaidanover" class="mw-redirect" title="Tzvi Hirsch Kaidanover">Tzvi Hirsch Kaidanover</a> (1648–1712), that when the angels appointed to bring infirmity and sickness upon people behold the angel Raphael, they take fright and flee. Then Raphael extends healing to the invalid.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the Beginning of Wisdom, an introduction to <a href="/wiki/Kabbalah" title="Kabbalah">kabbalistic</a> thought composed by Rabbi Aharon Meir Altshuler (1835–1905) in <a href="/wiki/Warsaw" title="Warsaw">Warsaw</a> between 1887–1893; Raphael is said to correspond to the <a href="/wiki/Sefirot" title="Sefirot">Sephirah</a> of <a href="/wiki/Tiferet" title="Tiferet">Tiphereth</a> (Beauty).<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup> He is said to act as an intermediate conduct between <a href="/wiki/Chesed" title="Chesed">Chesed</a> (Kindness)<sup id="cite_ref-32" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> corresponding to <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Gevurah" title="Gevurah">Din</a> (Judgement)<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> corresponding to <a href="/wiki/Gabriel" title="Gabriel">Gabriel</a>. <a href="/wiki/Uriel" title="Uriel">Uriel</a> (alternatively named <a href="/wiki/Nuriel" title="Nuriel">Nuriel</a>) is also said to act as an intermediate conduct alongside Raphael. It is further explained that when he inclines toward Chesed he is called Uriel, but when he inclines toward Din he is called Nuriel.<sup id="cite_ref-34" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> In the same context, the <a href="/wiki/Sefer_HaBahir" class="mw-redirect" title="Sefer HaBahir">Sefer HaBahir</a> calls Raphael the "Prince of Peace" (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: שַׂר־שָׁלוֹם <i>Śar Šālōm</i>). The text states that his reconciliation between Michael, the prince to God's right, and Gabriel, the prince to God's left is the meaning of the verse, "He imposes peace in His heights" (Job 25:2). With Raphael being the Archangel of Air that establishes peace between Fire and Water.<sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In kabbalistic <a href="/wiki/Astrology" title="Astrology">astrology</a>, Raphael is most commonly associated with the <a href="/wiki/Sun" title="Sun">Sun</a> (alongside Michael) and the planet <a href="/wiki/Mercury_(planet)" title="Mercury (planet)">Mercury</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Zohar" title="Zohar">Zohar</a> also associates him with the image of man in the <a href="/wiki/Tetramorph" title="Tetramorph">tetramorph</a> of the four holy <a href="/wiki/Living_creatures_(Bible)" title="Living creatures (Bible)">living creatures</a> of the Prophet <a href="/wiki/Ezekiel" title="Ezekiel">Ezekiel</a>'s vision, alongside the <a href="/wiki/Zodiac" title="Zodiac">zodiac</a> sign of <a href="/wiki/Aquarius_(astrology)" title="Aquarius (astrology)">Aquarius</a>, and in relation to the image of man; the Sephirah of <a href="/wiki/Malkuth" title="Malkuth">Malkhuth</a> (Kingdom)<sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Earth" title="Earth">Earth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> As well as Tiphereth and Malkhuth, the Zohar also has Raphael corresponding to the Sephirah of <a href="/wiki/Hod_(Kabbalah)" title="Hod (Kabbalah)">Hod</a> (Majesty),<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Euphrates" title="Euphrates">Euphrates</a> river (<a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: פְרָת <i>Pərāt</i>, <a href="/wiki/Tiberian_Hebrew" title="Tiberian Hebrew">Tiberian</a>: <i>Părāṯ;</i> the fourth river of <a href="/wiki/Garden_of_Eden" title="Garden of Eden">Eden</a> in <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Genesis" title="Book of Genesis">Genesis</a>) the left leg of the body, and the <a href="/wiki/Israelites" title="Israelites">Israelite</a> <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Tribes_of_Israel" title="Twelve Tribes of Israel">Tribe</a> of <a href="/wiki/Tribe_of_Ephraim" title="Tribe of Ephraim">Ephraim</a>. The text states: "The fourth (river) is Hod (Majesty), the "left leg" (referred to in what was said of <a href="/wiki/Jacob" title="Jacob">Jacob</a>, that "he halted on his left thigh"), and from it shall drink the camp of Raphael, whose mission is to heal the ills of the captivity, and with it the tribe of Ephraim and his two accompanying tribes (<a href="/wiki/Tribe_of_Manasseh" title="Tribe of Manasseh">Manasseh</a> and <a href="/wiki/Tribe_of_Benjamin" title="Tribe of Benjamin">Benjamin</a>)."<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>It is also customary in <a href="/wiki/Judaism" title="Judaism">Judaism</a> to invoke Raphael as one of the Four Archangels after one recites the <a href="/wiki/Shema_Yisrael" title="Shema Yisrael">Shema</a> before going to bed; with Michael by your right side, Gabriel by your left side, Uriel before you, and Raphael behind you.<sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> This practice is also referred to in <a href="/wiki/Rebbe" title="Rebbe">Rebbe</a> <a href="/wiki/Nachman_of_Breslov" title="Nachman of Breslov">Nachman of Breslov</a>'s (1772–1810) Likutei Etzot. In this work, he refers to the invocation of the Four <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">Archangels</a> as “<i>binding the chariot</i>”.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Christianity">Christianity</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Christianity">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg/220px-110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="277" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg/330px-110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg/440px-110.Tobias_and_the_Angel.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2304" data-file-height="2898" /></a><figcaption><i>Tobias and the Angel</i> by <a href="/wiki/Gustave_Dor%C3%A9" title="Gustave Doré">Gustave Doré</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a> names only two archangels or angels, Michael and Gabriel (Luke 1:9–26; Jude 1:9; Revelation 12:7), but Raphael, because of his association with healing, became identified with the unnamed angel of John 5:1–4 who periodically stirred the <a href="/wiki/Pool_of_Bethesda" title="Pool of Bethesda">pool of Bethesda</a> "[a]nd he that went down first into the pond after the motion of the water was made whole of whatsoever infirmity he lay under".<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> accordingly links Raphael with Michael and Gabriel as saints whose intercession can be sought through prayer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECresswell2011unpaginated_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECresswell2011unpaginated-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Patronage">Patronage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Patronage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG/193px-Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG" decoding="async" width="193" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG/290px-Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG/387px-Palazzo_Ducale_particolare.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1536" data-file-height="2048" /></a><figcaption>Raphael, Palazzo Ducale, Venice, detail</figcaption></figure>
<p>Due to his actions in the Book of Tobit and the Gospel of John, Saint Raphael is considered patron of travelers, the blind, happy meetings, nurses, physicians, medical workers, matchmakers,<sup id="cite_ref-47" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup> Christian marriage, and Catholic studies. As a particular enemy of the devil, he was revered in Catholic Europe as a special protector of sailors: on a corner of the famous <a href="/wiki/Doge%27s_Palace" title="Doge&#39;s Palace">Doge's Palace</a> in <a href="/wiki/Venice" title="Venice">Venice</a> is a relief depicting Raphael holding a scroll on which is written: "<i>Efficia fretum quietum</i>" (“Keep the <a href="/wiki/Gulf_of_Venice" title="Gulf of Venice">Gulf</a> quiet”). On July 8, 1497, when <a href="/wiki/Vasco_da_Gama" title="Vasco da Gama">Vasco da Gama</a> set sail from <a href="/wiki/Lisbon" title="Lisbon">Lisbon</a> with his four-ship fleet to India, the flagship was named <i>São Rafael</i> at the insistence of <a href="/wiki/List_of_Portuguese_monarchs" title="List of Portuguese monarchs">King</a> <a href="/wiki/Manuel_I_of_Portugal" title="Manuel I of Portugal">Manuel I of Portugal</a>. When the flotilla reached the <a href="/wiki/Cape_of_Good_Hope" title="Cape of Good Hope">Cape of Good Hope</a> on October 22, the sailors debarked and erected a column in the archangel's honor. The little statue of Raphael that accompanied Da Gama on the voyage is now in the Naval Museum in Lisbon.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Iconography">Iconography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Iconography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Raphael is said to guard pilgrims on their journeys, and is often depicted holding a staff. He is also often depicted holding or standing on a fish, which alludes to his healing of Tobit with the fish's gall.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> Early mosaics often show him and the other archangels in the clothing of a Byzantine courtier.<sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Feast_day">Feast day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Feast day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The feast day of Raphael was included for the first time in the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a> in 1921, for celebration on October 24. With the <a href="/wiki/Mysterii_Paschalis" title="Mysterii Paschalis">1969 revision</a> of the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar" title="General Roman Calendar">General Roman Calendar</a>, the feast was transferred to September 29 for celebration together with archangels Saints Michael and Gabriel.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup>  
<a href="/wiki/Pope_Benedict_XVI" title="Pope Benedict XVI">Pope Benedict XVI</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Summorum_Pontificum" title="Summorum Pontificum">Summorum Pontificum</a></i> permitted, within certain limits for public use, the <a href="/wiki/General_Roman_Calendar_of_1960" title="General Roman Calendar of 1960">General Roman Calendar of 1960</a>, which has October 24 as Raphael's feast day.
</p><p>He is commemorated in some Spanish dioceses on the old date<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> and with a procession on 7 May in Cordoba.<sup id="cite_ref-52" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the diocese of <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Warszawa-Praga" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Diocese of Warszawa-Praga">Diocese of Warszawa-Praga</a> he is commemorated on 3 October (with <a href="/wiki/Michael_(archangel)" title="Michael (archangel)">Michael</a> and <a href="/wiki/Gabriel" title="Gabriel">Gabriel</a>); the feast was transferred from 29 September (<a href="/wiki/Dedication_(ritual)" title="Dedication (ritual)">Dedication</a> of <a href="/wiki/St._Florian%27s_Cathedral" title="St. Florian&#39;s Cathedral">St. Florian's Cathedral</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The archangel Raphael is <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">commemorated</a> by the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> on 8 November in the Synaxis of the Archangel Michael and the Other Bodiless Powers.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches" title="Oriental Orthodox Churches">Oriental Orthodox Churches</a> dedication of church of Saint Raphael on the back of a whale is commemorated on 26 August (3 <a href="/wiki/Pagume" class="mw-redirect" title="Pagume">Pagumen</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-55" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Coptic_Orthodox_Church" title="Coptic Orthodox Church">Coptic Orthodox Church</a> celebrates Raphael's feast on <a href="/wiki/Intercalary_month_(Egypt)" title="Intercalary month (Egypt)">Kouji Nabot</a> 3<sup id="cite_ref-auto_1-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-auto-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Koiak" title="Koiak">Koiak</a> 13.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Antiochian_Western_Rite_Vicariate" title="Antiochian Western Rite Vicariate">Antiochian Western Rite Vicariate</a> Saint Raphael the Archangel is commemorated on 24 October.<sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Apparitions">Apparitions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Apparitions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Archangel Raphael is said to have appeared in <a href="/wiki/C%C3%B3rdoba,_Spain" title="Córdoba, Spain">Cordoba</a>, Spain, during the 16th century; in response to the city's appeal, <a href="/wiki/Pope_Innocent_X" title="Pope Innocent X">Pope Innocent X</a> allowed the local celebration of a feast in the Archangel's honor on May 7, the date of the principal apparition. Saint <a href="/wiki/John_of_God" title="John of God">John of God</a>, founder of <a href="/wiki/Brothers_Hospitallers_of_Saint_John_of_God" title="Brothers Hospitallers of Saint John of God">the Hospital order that bears his name</a>, is also said to have received visitations from Saint Raphael, who encouraged and instructed him. In tribute to this, many of the Brothers Hospitallers of St. John of God's facilities are called "Raphael Centers" to this day. The 18th century Neapolitan nun, Saint <a href="/wiki/Mary_Frances_of_the_Five_Wounds" title="Mary Frances of the Five Wounds">Maria Francesca of the Five Wounds</a>, is also said to have seen apparitions of Raphael.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Islam">In Islam</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: In Islam">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Arabic-manuscript.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Arabic-manuscript.jpg/170px-Arabic-manuscript.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="250" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Arabic-manuscript.jpg/255px-Arabic-manuscript.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/ce/Arabic-manuscript.jpg/340px-Arabic-manuscript.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2723" data-file-height="4000" /></a><figcaption><i>The Archangel Israfil</i>, made in Egypt or Syria, late 14th–early 15th century</figcaption></figure>
<p>Raphael (<a href="/wiki/Arabic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Arabic language">Arabic</a>: <span lang="ar" dir="rtl">إسرافيل</span>, <small><a href="/wiki/Romanization_of_Arabic" title="Romanization of Arabic">romanized</a>:&#160;</small><span title="Arabic-language romanization"><i lang="ar-Latn">ʾIsrāfīl</i></span>, alternate spellings: <i>Israfel</i>, <i>Esrafil</i>)<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2022)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> is a venerated <a href="/wiki/Archangel" title="Archangel">archangel</a> according to Islamic tradition. In <a href="/wiki/Islamic_eschatology" title="Islamic eschatology">Islamic eschatology</a>, Israfil will blow the trumpet from a holy rock in Jerusalem to announce the Day of Judgment (<i>Yawm al-Qiyāmah</i>). The trumpet is constantly poised at his lips, ready to be blown when God so orders.<sup id="cite_ref-israfil_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-israfil-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The name "Israfil" (or "Israfel", "Esrafil") is not specifically written in the <a href="/wiki/Quran" title="Quran">Quran</a>, although there is mention of an unnamed trumpet-angel assumed to identify this figure:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>And the trumpet shall be blown, so all those that are in the heavens and all those that are in the earth shall swoon, except him whom Allah will; then it shall be blown again, then they shall stand up awaiting.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8202;<cite>Qur'an (39.68)</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>Certain Islamic sources indicate that, created at the beginning of time, Israfil possesses four wings, and is so tall as to be able to reach from the earth to the pillars of <a href="/wiki/Heaven" title="Heaven">heaven</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> A beautiful angel who is a master of music, Israfil sings praises to God in a thousand different languages, the breath of which is used to inject life into hosts of angels who add to the songs themselves.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> Further he is probably the highest angel, since he also mediates between God and the other archangels, reading on the <a href="/wiki/Tablet_(religious)#In_Islam" title="Tablet (religious)">Preserved Tablet (<i>al-lawh al-mahfooz</i>)</a> to transmit the commands of God.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Although disputed, some reports assert he visited <a href="/wiki/Muhammad_in_Islam" title="Muhammad in Islam">Muhammad</a> prior to the archangel Gabriel.<sup id="cite_ref-61" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to <a href="/wiki/Sufi" class="mw-redirect" title="Sufi">Sufi</a> traditions reported by Imam Rafa'il, the Ghawth or <a href="/wiki/Qutb" title="Qutb">Qutb</a> ('perfect human being'), is someone who has a heart that resembles that of the archangel Israfil, signifying the loftiness of this angel. The next in rank are the saints who are known as the Umdah or Awtad, amongst whom the highest ones have their hearts resembling that of archangel Mikhail (archangel Michael), and the rest of the lower ranking saints having the heart of Jibrail (archangel Gabriel), and that of the previous prophets before the Islamic <a href="/wiki/Prophet" title="Prophet">prophet</a> <a href="/wiki/Muhammad" title="Muhammad">Muhammad</a>. The earth is believed to always have one of the Qutb.<sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In another account, Rafāʾīl (Arabic: رفائيل) is mentioned by name in the Islamic tradition narrated by Ath-Tha'labi from <a href="/wiki/Ali" title="Ali">Ali</a>. He is said to have met <a href="/wiki/Dhu_al-Qarnayn" title="Dhu al-Qarnayn">Dhu al-Qarnayn</a> who is mentioned in the last part of Surah 18 of the Quran, <a href="/wiki/Al-Kahf" title="Al-Kahf">al-Kahf</a> ("The Cave"). <a href="/wiki/Dhu_al-Qarnayn" title="Dhu al-Qarnayn">Dhu al-Qarnayn</a> (The Two Horned One) is believed by some to be <a href="/wiki/Alexander_The_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Alexander The Great">Alexander The Great</a>. 
</p><p>The angel told <a href="/wiki/Dhu_al-Qarnayn" title="Dhu al-Qarnayn">Dhu al-Qarnayn</a> about the Water of Life (Ayn al-Hayat). Hearing that there was such a spring, <a href="/wiki/Dhu_al-Qarnayn" title="Dhu al-Qarnayn">Dhu al-Qarnayn</a> wanted to drink the Water of Life, but the only one who had succeeded in drinking it was his cousin, <a href="/wiki/Khidr" title="Khidr">Khidr</a>. In Islamic tradition, <a href="/wiki/Khidr" title="Khidr">Khidr</a> is the mystical guide popularly quoted especially in <a href="/wiki/Sufi" class="mw-redirect" title="Sufi">Sufi</a> traditions who has attained a long life and appears to selected Islamic saints throughout the times.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Places_named_for_Raphael">Places named for Raphael</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Places named for Raphael">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The following places have been named in honor of Raphael:
</p><p>Saint Raphaël, France; Saint Raphaël, Quebec, Canada; and San Rafaels in Argentina, Bolivia, Colombia, Costa Rica, Chile, Mexico, Trinidad and Tobago, Peru, and the Philippines; also San Rafael de El Moján and San Rafael de Orituco in Venezuela.
</p>
<ul><li>In the United States, patronages under the name San Rafael, inherited from Spanish tradition survive in <a href="/wiki/California" title="California">California</a> (where besides <a href="/wiki/San_Rafael,_California" title="San Rafael, California">the city</a> there are the <a href="/wiki/San_Rafael_Mountains" title="San Rafael Mountains">San Rafael Mountains</a>) and also:</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Mission_San_Rafael_Arc%C3%A1ngel" title="Mission San Rafael Arcángel">Mission San Rafael Arcángel</a> in <a href="/wiki/San_Rafael,_California" title="San Rafael, California">San Rafael, California</a>.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/New_Mexico" title="New Mexico">New Mexico</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Utah" title="Utah">Utah</a>, where the <a href="/wiki/San_Rafael_River" title="San Rafael River">San Rafael River</a> flows seasonally in the <a href="/wiki/San_Rafael_Desert" class="mw-redirect" title="San Rafael Desert">San Rafael Desert</a>.</li></ul>
<p>More recent examples include:
</p>
<ul><li>St. Raphael's Cathedral, the seat of the <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Madison" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Madison">Diocese of Madison</a>, Wisconsin</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St._Raphael%27s_Cathedral_(Dubuque)" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Raphael&#39;s Cathedral (Dubuque)">St. Raphael's Cathedral</a>, the seat of the <a href="/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Dubuque" class="mw-redirect" title="Archdiocese of Dubuque">Archdiocese of Dubuque</a></li>
<li>St. Raphael's Episcopal Church in Crossville, Tennessee, in the Episcopal Diocese of East Tennessee.</li>
<li>St. Raphael the Archangel Catholic Church and School, Raleigh, North Carolina</li>
<li>St. Raphael the Archangel Parish, Saint Louis, Missouri</li>
<li>St. Raphael Hospital New Haven, Connecticut, USA</li></ul>
<p>Elsewhere:
</p>
<ul><li>St. Raphael’s Catholic Church, Yeading, Hayes, Middlesex, <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a></li>
<li>St. Raphael's Church, <a href="/wiki/Huccaby" class="mw-redirect" title="Huccaby">Huccaby</a>, Hexworthy, Dartmoor National Park, Devon, <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a></li>
<li>San Rafael, <a href="/wiki/Vecindario" title="Vecindario">Vecindario</a>, Santa Lucia de Tirajana <a href="/wiki/Gran_Canaria" title="Gran Canaria">Gran Canaria</a></li></ul>
<p>The Arcangelo Raffaello youth confraternity functioned in Florence, Italy from its founding in 1411 to its suppression in 1785.<sup id="cite_ref-63" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>St. John of God Catholic Church in Chicago, IL, was disassembled, moved and reassembled as St. Raphael the Archangel Church in Mill Creek, IL.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_popular_culture">In popular culture</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Raphael_(archangel)&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: In popular culture">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Raphael, along with many other prominent angels, appears in <a href="/wiki/John_Milton" title="John Milton">John Milton</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Paradise_Lost" title="Paradise Lost">Paradise Lost</a></i>, in which he is assigned by God to re-warn Adam concerning the sin of eating of the <a href="/wiki/Tree_of_the_knowledge_of_good_and_evil" title="Tree of the knowledge of good and evil">tree of the knowledge of good and evil</a>. He also expounds to Adam the War in Heaven in which <a href="/wiki/Lucifer" title="Lucifer">Lucifer</a> and the demons fell, and the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Earth" title="Earth">Earth</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Joseph_Haydn" title="Joseph Haydn">Joseph Haydn</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/The_Creation_(Haydn)" title="The Creation (Haydn)">Creation</a></i>, Raphael (bass) is one of the three angelic narrators, along with Gabriel (soprano) and Uriel (tenor).
</p><p>Characters associated with Angelic figures, at least by borrowing the names, also feature in the video game industry, as an example there is the title: <i><a href="/wiki/El_Shaddai:_Ascension_of_the_Metatron" title="El Shaddai: Ascension of the Metatron">El Shaddai: Ascension of the Metatron</a></i>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (April 2019)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>Raphael features as one of the four archangels in the TV series <i><a href="/wiki/Supernatural_(U.S._TV_series)" class="mw-redirect" title="Supernatural (U.S. TV series)">Supernatural</a></i>.
</p><p>In season 2 of the TV series <i><a href="/wiki/Criminal_Minds" title="Criminal Minds">Criminal Minds</a></i> the archangel Raphael is brought up as being one of Tobias Hankel's personalities.
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/The_Yogscast" class="mw-redirect" title="The Yogscast">Yogscast</a> YouTube series <i>Shadow of Israphel</i>, the main antagonist and titular character derives his namesake from that of St. Raphael.
</p>

<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">

<li id="cite_note-25"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-25">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><span lang="Hebrew" dir="rtl">לביאל יפה ראית לעצמך שריפאת מה שהרסו חבריך, מיד החליף את שמו וקרא אותו רפאל ומסר בידו כל מיני רפואות שבעולם.</span>"Beautiful Libbiel, you saw for yourself that you healed what your friends destroyed." He immediately changed his name and called him Raphael, and He delivered in his hand all the types of medicines that are in the Universe. – Midrash Konen 1:9.</span>
